### 10. cyprian-of-carthage — Cyprian of Carthage

**Checked:** Surface A — every field of `content/giao-phu/cyprian-of-carthage.json` including all six `sections[]` bodies and both `apologetics[]` items: the headline quote traced to the Latin (*Habere non potest Deum patrem qui ecclesiam non habet matrem*) and to ch. 6; the Augustine quotation inside `later-influence` traced to *De baptismo* I.18.28 (EWTN + New Advent NPNF); the *Acta Proconsularia* details checked line-by-line against an English text of the *Acta*; dates against the 1913 CE, Britannica and Benedict XVI's 2007 catechesis; the *De unitate* ch. 4 two-recension problem researched and reported with both sides; the Stephen I breach checked against Firmilian *Ep.* 74 §§25–26; feast day against the current General Roman Calendar; Vietnamese spelling against the site's own corpus and Giáo phận Vĩnh Long; both legacy combined datasets checked for contradictions. Surface B — the one Cyprian citation in `giao-hoi-so-khai-co-cau-nguyen-voi-cac-thanh.md` line 25 checked against what Cyprian's letters actually say (*Ep.* 56[60].5 and *Ep.* 33[39].3), plus `refs_ccc` 957/2683 at vatican.va.

#### Surface A — Giáo Phụ entry (`content/giao-phu/cyprian-of-carthage.json`)

| Field | Claim as written | What the sources say | Verdict | Sources |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `dates.display` / `dates.born` vs `facts[]` "Born" | `display` "c. 200 — 258", `born: 200` — but `facts[]` Born says "c. 200/210 AD" / "khoảng năm 200/210" | **Internally inconsistent, and `display` is falsely precise.** The 1913 CE: "Of the date of the saint's birth and of his early life **nothing is known**." Britannica: birth date unknown, "believed to be about 200". Benedict XVI's catechesis on Cyprian says he converted **at age 35** and that there were **13 years between his conversion and his martyrdom** — which, working back from 258, puts his birth c. **210**, not 200. So c. 200 and c. 210 are both live and no source can settle it. | `contested` → make `display` match `facts[]`: `"c. 200/210 — 258"`; leave `born` as the machine sort key but do not present 200 as a date. Fixing this also resolves the Surface B clash (see below). | [Catholic Encyclopedia, St. Cyprian](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/04583b.htm), [Britannica, St. Cyprian](https://www.britannica.com/biography/Saint-Cyprian-Christian-bishop), [Benedict XVI, General Audience 6 June 2007 (vatican.va)](https://www.vatican.va/content/benedict-xvi/en/audiences/2007/documents/hf_ben-xvi_aud_20070606.html) |
| `sections[id=later-influence]` + `apologetics[1]` | "in real if painful disagreement with Rome, **without ever being formally corrected or disciplined for it**" / VI "mà không bao giờ bị chính thức sửa sai hay kỷ luật vì điều đó"; and "**without that amounting to schism**" | Technically defensible on the narrow point (no source records a formal excommunication of Cyprian, and Stephen died in Aug 257 before anything was concluded) — but as written it **substantially understates how sharp the breach got**, which is exactly what a Protestant reader will press. Firmilian, *Ep.* 74(75) §25–26, says Stephen refused even to grant the African bishops' delegation "the speech of an ordinary conference," and "commanded the entire fraternity, that no one should receive them into his house" (i.e. denial of hospitality/communion), and §26 that Stephen dared "call Cyprian a false Christ and a false apostle, and a deceitful worker." Stephen also cut off communion with Helenus of Tarsus, Firmilian and the churches of Cilicia and Cappadocia (Dionysius of Alexandria, in Eusebius, *HE*). **How far this went — a formal excommunication of the Africans or "only" a refusal of communion/hospitality — is genuinely disputed and the primary evidence is one-sided (Firmilian is a hostile witness).** | `contested` → keep the claim but qualify it, e.g. VI: `"…ngài qua đời khi vẫn giữ lập trường của mình. Sự bất đồng đã đi rất xa: theo lá thư của Firmilianô (Thư 74), Đức Stêphanô đã từ chối tiếp phái đoàn giám mục Phi châu và ra lệnh không ai được đón tiếp họ. Tuy nhiên không có tài liệu nào cho thấy chính Cyprianô bị dứt phép thông công cách chính thức, và Đức Stêphanô qua đời năm 257 trước khi sự việc ngã ngũ. Mức độ đoạn giao thực sự đến đâu vẫn còn được các học giả tranh luận."` | [Firmilian, *Ep.* 74 (New Advent, ANF)](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/050674.htm), [Catholic Encyclopedia, Firmilian](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/06080b.htm), [Encyclopedia.com, Stephen I, Pope, St.](https://www.encyclopedia.com/religion/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/stephen-i-pope-st) |
| `sections[id=later-influence]` — **quotation inside quotation marks** | "**the brilliant sunshine of his glorious blood**" / VI "ánh nắng rực rỡ của dòng máu vinh quang của ngài" — attributed to Augustine, with **no citation given** | The passage is real but the entry's wording is **not any standard translation**. Augustine, *De baptismo contra Donatistas* **I.18.28**, reads (NPNF/EWTN): "**if any cloud had crept over the clearness of his intellect from his infirmity as man, it might be dispelled by the glorious brightness of his blood**." "Brilliant sunshine" does not appear; the metaphor is *brightness/light*, not sunshine. | `corrected` → use the attested rendering and add the citation. VI e.g. `"nếu vì sự yếu đuối phàm nhân mà có đám mây nào phủ mờ sự sáng suốt của trí ngài, thì đám mây ấy đã được xua tan bởi ánh sáng rực rỡ của dòng máu ngài" (Augustinô, Về Phép Rửa, chống phái Đônatô, I,18,28)` | [Augustine, *On Baptism* I.18.28 (EWTN)](https://www.ewtn.com/catholicism/library/on-baptism-against-the-donatists-books-iiv-11745), [same, New Advent](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/14081.htm) |
| `sections[id=later-influence]` | "The question was **effectively settled a century and a half later by Augustine of Hippo**" | Overstated. The *decision* long predates Augustine: the **Council of Arles (314), canon 8** already ruled that a convert baptized in the name of Father, Son and Holy Spirit receives only the laying on of hands and is **not** rebaptized — and Rome's practice was settled from Stephen onward. Augustine (writing c. 400–401 against the Donatists) supplied the **theological rationale** that became standard and the way of honouring Cyprian while rejecting his view — he did not settle the question. | `corrected` → e.g. "The question had already been decided against Cyprian's practice by the Council of Arles in 314; what Augustine did, a century and a half later, was supply the reasoning that became the standard Catholic explanation…" | [Council of Arles 314, canon 8 (Fourth Century Christianity)](https://www.fourthcentury.com/arles-314-canons/), [Denzinger, Arles I — Baptism of heretics](https://sensusfidelium.com/the-sources-of-catholic-dogma-the-denzinger/st-sylvester-i-314-335-council-of-arelas-i-314-the-baptism-of-heretics/) |
| `sections[id=martyrdom]` | "**there is no meaningful historical doubt** about the date, the charge, or the manner of his death" / VI "không có sự hoài nghi lịch sử đáng kể nào…" | The **core is genuinely well attested** — 14 September 258, beheading by the sword, sentence of the proconsul **Galerius Maximus** at the Villa Sexti, under Valerian's second edict — and the *Acta* rest on official court reports. But "no meaningful doubt" is stronger than the text supports: the *Passio/Acta Cypriani* is a **composite of three separate documents** (the 257 trial before Aspasius Paternus and exile to Curubis; the 258 arrest and trial; the execution narrative), joined by editorial connecting phrases, and it survives only in versions "prepared with a view to the edification of the faithful." The narrative colouring (the 25 gold pieces, the disrobing) is on weaker footing than the date and sentence. | `contested` → soften to something like VI: "các chi tiết chính — ngày tháng, tội danh và cách thức hành quyết — được các sử gia xem là đáng tin cậy khác thường đối với một trình thuật tử đạo, vì phần lớn dựa trên biên bản chính thức; bản văn hiện còn là một tập hợp ba tài liệu được biên soạn lại, nên một vài chi tiết kể chuyện thì kém chắc chắn hơn." | [*Acts of the Martyrs*, Catholic Encyclopedia (composite of three documents)](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/09742b.htm), [Acta proconsularia Cypriani, Oxford Early Christian Texts (BiblIndex)](https://www.biblindex.org/en/work-editions/acta-proconsularia-cypriani/oxford-early-christian-texts), [Catholic Encyclopedia, St. Cyprian](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/04583b.htm) |
| `sections[id=context]` (minor) | "Decius required **every citizen** empire-wide to publicly sacrifice" | The decree required **all inhabitants** of the empire to sacrifice before a magistrate and obtain a signed, witnessed *libellus* — not only citizens. Also worth knowing (not required in the text): modern scholarship (J. B. **Rives**, "The Decree of Decius and the Religion of Empire", *JRS* 1999) argues the decree was **not necessarily aimed at Christians** but at restoring empire-wide sacrifice; the entry does not claim otherwise, so no correction is needed there. | `corrected` (minor) → "every inhabitant of the empire" / VI "mọi cư dân trong đế quốc" | [Rives, *The Decree of Decius and the Religion of Empire*, JRS (Cambridge)](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/journal-of-roman-studies/article/abs/decree-of-decius-and-the-religion-of-empire/CBAD383D53A66A01EB67E75022C373E2), [Judaism and Rome, Papyrus Rylands 112a (libellus)](https://www.judaism-and-rome.org/papyrus-rylands-112a) |
| `sections[id=more-quotes]` — Acta details | "describes him **removing his own outer garments without assistance**, **having his eyes bound by his own clergy**, and giving twenty-five gold pieces to his executioner **before kneeling to pray** and being beheaded" | The 25 gold pieces, "Deo gratias", the sword sentence and the proconsul **Galerius Maximus** are all correct. Two details are inverted: (a) the *Acta* has him **bind his own eyes** and only then get help — "the great Cyprian with his own hands bandaged his eyes, but since he could not tie the corners of the handkerchief, presbyter Julian and subdeacon Julian went to help him"; the *clothing* is what he handed to his clergy ("removed his dalmatic and gave it to the deacons, leaving himself only in his linen garment"). (b) The **prayer/kneeling comes before the disrobing**, not after. (Note the 1913 CE gives the two helpers as "a priest and a deacon, both called Julius"; the *Acta* text gives presbyter and *subdeacon* Julian — a minor source divergence.) | `corrected` → swap the two details: he removed his own outer garments and **handed them to his clergy**, prayed prostrate, and **bound his own eyes, his clergy helping tie the knot**. | [*Acta Proconsularia Cypriani*, English text](https://silouanthompson.net/2007/07/cyprianmartyrdom/), [Catholic Encyclopedia, St. Cyprian](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/04583b.htm) |
| `sections[id=theology]`, opening clause | "*On the Unity of the Catholic Church* was written for a specific crisis, **a rival bishop set up in Carthage by rigorists angry at Cyprian's moderate handling of the lapsed**" / VI "một giám mục đối lập được nhóm **cực đoan** dựng lên tại Carthage vì tức giận trước cách xử lý **ôn hòa** của Cyprianô đối với những người sa ngã" | **Inverted, on both halves.** (a) The Carthaginian party behind the schism *De unitate* addresses was the **laxist** one — the deacon Felicissimus and five presbyters headed by Novatus, who were reconciling the lapsed indiscriminately **without penance** and were angry that Cyprian was *too strict/too slow*, not too moderate. The **rigorists** (Novatian at Rome; his legate **Maximus** later set up as anti-bishop at Carthage) are a different party. (b) The **rival bishop** of the laxist party, **Fortunatus**, was consecrated **in 252** — *after* the treatise, which Cyprian read to the council of Carthage in **spring/April 251**. So no rival bishop was yet in place at Carthage when it was written. | `corrected` → e.g. EN: "…written for a specific crisis: a schism at Carthage led by the deacon Felicissimus and a group of presbyters who wanted the lapsed readmitted at once without penance, and who rejected Cyprian's authority; the treatise was read to the council of Carthage in 251." VI correspondingly, replacing "nhóm cực đoan"/"ôn hòa" with the laxist party and Cyprian's *strictness*. | [Catholic Encyclopedia, St. Cyprian](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/04583b.htm), [Schaff, *History of the Christian Church* II §](https://www.ccel.org/ccel/schaff/hcc2.v.vi.xviii.html), [Wace, *Dict. of Early Christian Biography*: Felicissimus](https://www.studylight.org/dictionaries/eng/hwd/f/felicissimus-deacon-of-carthage.html) |
| `sections[id=theology]` (same clause), secondary | implies a single, settled occasion for the treatise | Worth knowing: **scholars disagree** about whether *De unitate* also targets **Novatian's** schism at Rome. One line of scholarship holds news of Novatian reached Carthage only at the moment of the council, too late for a treatise read to it; others read the treatise (and especially the second recension of ch. 4) as anti-Novatianist. Best not to state a single target flatly. | `contested` | [Concordia Seminary, *Double Edition of De unitate ecclesiae*](https://scholar.csl.edu/bdiv/455/), [Catholic Encyclopedia](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/04583b.htm) |
| `works[]` *De unitate* / `sections[id=theology]` — **OMISSION, flagged per task brief** | The entry treats *De unitate* as a single stable text and never mentions the textual problem. | *De unitate* survives in **two recensions**, differing chiefly at **ch. 4** (and ch. 19): the **"Primacy Text" (PT)**, which grounds unity on Peter's "one chair" and a *primatus* given to Peter, and the **"Textus Receptus" (TR)**, which speaks of Peter only as the symbol of a unity all bishops share equally. This is directly load-bearing for papal primacy and has been argued over for centuries. **Both sides, fairly stated:** (i) the position associated with **Maurice Bévenot** (Oxford/Clarendon critical edition, 1971; CCSL 3) and now the majority view — *both* recensions are **Cyprian's own**, PT being the original and TR his own later revision (Bévenot linked the revision to the baptismal controversy with Stephen, i.e. Cyprian rewriting because Stephen was using PT against him); (ii) **Dom John Chapman's** variant — Cyprian wrote TR first (against Felicissimus/Novatus) and PT second, for the Novatian controversy; (iii) the older **interpolation thesis**, that PT is a **later Roman forgery/interpolation**, not Cyprian's. A separate passage at the start of ch. 5 is now generally agreed to be Cyprian's own later addition. **The audit's instruction is to flag and represent both sides, not to pick one.** | `contested` → add an explicit note in the `theology` (or `works_note`) section, e.g. VI: `"Chương 4 của tác phẩm này còn lại trong hai bản khác nhau — bản gọi là 'Primacy Text' nhấn mạnh 'một tòa' của Phêrô, và bản 'Textus Receptus' thì không. Đa số học giả ngày nay (theo M. Bévenot) cho rằng cả hai đều do chính Cyprianô viết, bản sau là do chính ngài sửa lại; một số học giả khác vẫn cho bản dài hơn là do người sau thêm vào. Vấn đề chưa được giải quyết dứt khoát [cần kiểm chứng thêm]."` | [Concordia Seminary, *The Double Edition of St Cyprian's De unitate ecclesiae*](https://scholar.csl.edu/bdiv/455/), [Bévenot, *Cyprian: De Lapsis and De Ecclesiae Catholicae Unitate*, Clarendon 1971 (rev. in *Church History*)](https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/church-history/article/abs/cyprian-de-lapsis-and-de-ecclesiae-catholicae-unitate-edited-and-translated-by-maurice-bevenot-oxford-clarendon-press-1971-xxiii-127-pp-575-sacrauzentum-unitatis-ein-beitrag-zum-verstandnis-der-kirche-bei-cyprian-by-ulrich-wickert-berlin-and-new-york-walter-de-gruyter-1971-x-164-pp-58-dm/35F59934EFFD24DD8E11DDE42B895DE7), [Catholic Encyclopedia, St. Cyprian (§ on the two versions)](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/04583b.htm) |

**Checked and clean (no row needed):** `facts[]` Feast day "September 16 (with St. Cornelius)" — correct, obligatory memorial of Ss. Cornelius and Cyprian in the current General Roman Calendar (and the same date pre-1969; his *dies natalis* is 14 Sept, occupied by the Exaltation of the Cross). `facts[]` Canonized "Yes" — pre-Congregation, universally venerated. `role` Bishop of Carthage · Martyr — correct; he is **not** a Doctor of the Church and the entry does not claim he is. `era` ante-Nicene — correct. `life[]` conversion "c. 246" and "elected bishop only about two years after his own baptism" — supported (baptism probably Easter 246; bishop c. 248/early 249; Pontius, *Vita* 5, stresses he was made bishop "while still a neophyte"). `sections[id=context]` care for pagans during the plague — supported by Pontius, *Vita* 9. `ccc_refs` — empty. `works[]` Latin titles *De Unitate Ecclesiae* / *De Lapsis* and the attributions — correct and undisputed. `sections[id=further-reading]` — G. W. Clarke, *The Letters of St. Cyprian of Carthage*, 4 vols., ACW, Paulist Press 1984–89, and Allen Brent, *On the Church: Select Letters*, PPS, SVS Press 2006, both exist as described.

That is a promise to *keep praying after death*, not an instruction to *address* the departed. (2) **Prayer and Eucharistic offering *for* the martyrs** — *Ep.* 33[39].3: "sacrifices for them we always, as you remember, offer, as often as we celebrate the passions and days of the martyrs in the annual commemoration." That is the opposite direction. **No surviving text of Cyprian invokes a saint or tells anyone to ask a saint in heaven to pray for them.** (Notably, the standard Catholic apologetics catena — churchfathers.org, "Intercession of the Saints" — cites Cyprian precisely at *Letters* 56[60]:5, i.e. for intercession-of-the-departed, not invocation; and the earliest text that clearly *prays to Cyprian* is **Gregory of Nazianzus, Or. 24** (c. 380), a century later.) A Protestant reader who checks will find the strongest Father in that list, on the actual claim made, is not Cyprian. | `corrected` (Cyprian is misassigned to the wrong claim, not fabricated) | Two options for Session 3. **(a) Keep him, relabel him** — split the list, e.g. line 22 → "Nhiều Giáo Phụ lớn cũng làm chứng cho niềm tin này. ### 11. anthony-the-great — Anthony the Great

**Checked:** `quote` traced verbatim to Antony 7 of the alphabetical *Apophthegmata Patrum*; the philosophers' saying in `sections[more-quotes]` traced to *Life of Antony* 73; the Augustine claim in `sections[later-influence]` traced to *Confessions* VIII.6.14–15 and the two early Latin versions of the *Life* to the Oxford Cult of Saints database; `dates` against ODCC, Britannica, Encyclopedia.com, the 1913 CE and the Coptic Synaxarium; the 338 Alexandria visit against the primary *Festal Index*; the seven Letters and the Rubenson debate against Rubenson and the 1913 CE; `sections[martyrdom]` (Maximin Daia 311, secret burial, Macarius and Amathas, the sheepskin) against *Life* 46 and 91–92; all Vietnamese proper names grepped across the site's own `content/` tree; the legacy `church-fathers.json` / `-vi.json` rows compared field by field. `ccc_refs` is empty.

| Field | Claim as written | What the sources say | Verdict | Sources |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `era` (both entries) | `era: "ante-nicene"` | The site's own `ERA_SPAN` in `lib/giao-phu/eras.ts` gives ante-Nicene as `c. 100 — 270` and Nicene as `c. 260 — 397`. Anthony (251–356) and Pachomius (c. 292–348) fall almost entirely outside the ante-Nicene span and squarely inside the Nicene one; both were adults living through and after Nicaea (325), and Anthony's public act on the record is his 338 anti-Arian visit to Alexandria. The legacy combined file classes both as `"Desert/Monastic Father"`, so the site is already internally inconsistent about them. Not a *technical* misuse of a defined corpus (unlike "Apostolic Fathers"), so this is a soft-grouping judgement, but the label contradicts the site's own published span. | `corrected` → propose `era: "nicene"` for both (or a documented editorial note that the monastic figures are grouped by their pre-Nicene *roots*) | repo: `lib/giao-phu/eras.ts`, `content/giao-phu/church-fathers.json` |
| `life[1]` / legacy `known_for` — "Emerged from the desert in 338 AD to publicly support Athanasius against Arianism" | 338 | **Correct, and worth protecting.** The *Festal Index* to Athanasius's Festal Letters, under Letter X (year 337–8), records: "Antony, the great leader, came to Alexandria, and though he remained there only two days, shewed himself wonderful in many things... He went away on the third of Messori (July 27, 338)." Note that the 1913 *Catholic Encyclopedia* dates this visit "c. 350" — a later corrector working from the open web may well try to "fix" 338 to 350. The Festal Index is the primary document and 338 is right. | `confirmed` (flagged so it is not "corrected" later) | [Festal Index, NPNF2-04 (ccel)](https://www.ccel.org/ccel/schaff/npnf204.xxv.iii.ii.html), [1913 CE, St. Anthony](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/01553d.htm) |
| `dates.display` / `dates.born` (No. 11) | `"251 — 356"`, `born: 251` — a bare year, no "c." | **Textbook false precision.** No ancient source gives Anthony a birth year at all. The year 251 is a *back-computation*: Athanasius's *Life* says only that he died "at the age of a hundred and five," and 356 − 105 = 251. Reference works accordingly all hedge: the *Oxford Dictionary of the Christian Church* prints "(251?–356)"; Britannica gives "born c. 251"; the Concise Oxford Dictionary of World Religions (via Encyclopedia.com) gives "c. 250–356"; the 1913 *Catholic Encyclopedia* declines a year entirely — "born at Coma… about the middle of the third century." | `contested` → propose `display: "c. 251 — 356"` and Vietnamese `"kh. 251 — 356"` in the entry's style (keep `born: 251` as the machine value) | [ODCC / Episcopal Church glossary](https://www.episcopalchurch.org/glossary/antony/), [Britannica](https://www.britannica.com/biography/Saint-Anthony-of-Egypt), [Encyclopedia.com (Concise Oxford Dict. of World Religions)](https://www.encyclopedia.com/religion/dictionaries-thesauruses-pictures-and-press-releases/antony-st), [1913 CE](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/01553d.htm) |

January 12 appears to be a modern web artifact (it is in the Wikipedia infobox) with no patristic warrant. The site is also the only place that carries it forward; the legacy `church-fathers.json` repeats the same string, so both files need the same fix. | `unverifiable` → propose replacing with `"khoảng năm 251"` / `"c. 251 AD"`, or flagging `[cần kiểm chứng]` | [Coptic Synaxarium, Toba 22](https://www.copticchurch.net/synaxarium/5_22.html?lang=en), [Britannica](https://www.britannica.com/biography/Saint-Anthony-of-Egypt) |

A biography *written about* Anthony by Athanasius cannot be one of Anthony's own surviving works. What actually survives under Anthony's name: **seven Letters** (Jerome, *De viris illustribus* 88, already knew "seven letters… translated from the Coptic into Greek"; they survive fragmentarily in Coptic, completely in Georgian and in a Latin version, with an Arabic corpus of twenty that is "by common consent pronounced wholly spurious"), plus a Greek letter to Theodore, plus the ~38 sayings under his name in the alphabetical *Apophthegmata Patrum*. | `corrected` → remove *The Life of Antony* from `works[]` (or move it to `works_note` as "the primary source, written **about** him by Athanasius"), and set the count to `"7 lá thư (tính xác thực được tranh luận)"` / `"7 letters (authenticity debated)"` — not a bare "2 works" | [Rubenson, *Letters of St Antony* (Internet Archive)](https://archive.org/details/lettersofstanton0000rube), [1913 CE, St. Anthony](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/01553d.htm) |

One small precision: the *Coptic* survives only in **fragments** — the complete texts are the Georgian and Latin. ### 12. pachomius — Pachomius the Great

**Checked:** `quote` searched for in Veilleux's *Pachomian Koinonia* I, Schodde's Ethiopic *Rules* and the open web (not found); the angel's reply in `sections[more-quotes]` traced verbatim to Schodde's 1885 translation and its true source identified as Palladius; `dates`, the monastery/monk counts, the founding year, Jerome's 404 translation, the four Praecepta collections, the eleven Letters and the Instructions all against the *Coptic Encyclopedia* article by Armand Veilleux, Palladius's *Lausiac History* 7 and 32, the New Catholic Encyclopedia and Britannica; the Basil claim against Britannica; feast day against the Roman Martyrology tradition and the Coptic calendar (14 Bashans); "Pacômiô" checked against hdgmvietnam.com and tgpsaigon.net. `ccc_refs` is empty.

| `dates.display` / `dates.died` / `facts[]` "Qua đời / Died" (No. 12) | `"c. 292 — 348"`, `died: 348`, facts row `"năm 348"` / `"348 AD"` — a bare year, no hedge | **Genuinely contested, and the entry contradicts itself.** Its own `sections[martyrdom]` already says "he died in 346 or 348 (sources vary)" — but `dates` and `facts[]` print a bare 348. The *New Catholic Encyclopedia* gives **"293?–346"** and "Pachomius died on May 9, **346**"; the Roman Martyrology tradition likewise gives "died at Phbow around the year **346**". 348 is the figure carried by the popular web. The day (9 May) is firm; the year is not. | `contested` → propose `display: "kh. 292 — 346/348"` / `"c. 292 — 346/348"` and the `facts[]` Died row `"9 tháng 5, năm 346 hoặc 348"` / `"May 9, 346 or 348"`, matching what `sections[martyrdom]` already concedes | [New Catholic Encyclopedia via Encyclopedia.com](https://www.encyclopedia.com/environment/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/pachomius), [Catholic Culture, May 9](https://www.catholicculture.org/culture/liturgicalyear/calendar/day.cfm?date=2025-05-09) |

But the figures the sources actually give differ by more than a factor of two: **Palladius**, the earliest outside witness, calls Pachomius "archimandrite over **3000** men"; the *Coptic Encyclopedia* (Veilleux) says "In 346, when Pachomius died, there were some **5,000** monks in nine monasteries (plus the nuns in their two convents)"; the *New Catholic Encyclopedia* line is "perhaps three thousand." The ~7,000 figure is traceable: it is **Palladius, *Lausiac History* 32** — "There are many monasteries that have maintained this model, amounting to **seven thousand** men. The first and great monastery, where Pachomius himself lived… has one thousand three hundred men" — describing the federation **as Palladius knew it c. 419–420, some seventy years after Pachomius died**. (The Ethiopic recension of the same chapter reads 5,000 and 300 instead.) It is routinely retro-fitted onto Pachomius's lifetime across the popular web. Nothing supports it as an at-death number, and "over 7,000" overstates even the highest at-death estimate. | `contested` -> propose `"khoảng 3.000 đến 5.000 đan sĩ và nữ tu (các nguồn không thống nhất)"` / `"between about 3,000 and 5,000 monks and nuns (sources disagree)"`; do **not** print "over 7,000" | [Coptic Encyclopedia, PACHOMIUS (Veilleux)](https://ccdl.claremont.edu/digital/api/collection/cce/id/1497/download), [Palladius, *Lausiac History* (tertullian.org)](https://www.tertullian.org/fathers/palladius_lausiac_02_text.htm), [New Catholic Encyclopedia via Encyclopedia.com](https://www.encyclopedia.com/environment/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/pachomius), [Lausiac History 32 (catholiclibrary.org)](https://catholiclibrary.org/library/view?docId=%2FFathers-Synchronized-EN%2FPalladius_Helenopolitanus__Historia_Lausiaca.en.html&chunk.id=00000057) |

### 13. athanasius-of-alexandria — Athanasius of Alexandria

**Checked:** Headline quote traced to the NPNF Greek/English text of *De Incarnatione* 54 (= 54.3, PG 25:192B); the Jerome quotation in `more-quotes` traced to *Altercatio Luciferiani et Orthodoxi* ch. 19 (Latin and English both verified, plus what ch. 19 is actually about); all four `works[]` checked for authorship; dates checked against Britannica, the Erlangen *Athanasius-Werke* critical-edition project, the liturgical propers and the 1908/1913 Catholic Encyclopedia; the **five-exile count checked specifically** against the Erlangen project and the Roman-calendar propers; feast day against the current General Roman Calendar; Doctor status against the 1568 declaration; Tyre 335 (Arsenius, the grain-ship charge, Trier), the 8 Feb 356 Syrianus raid, the *Letters to Serapion*/Tropici, and the *Life of Antony*→Augustine link all verified; Vietnamese proper names grepped against `content/content.json` and checked at hdgmvietnam.com; cross-checked against `church-fathers.json` and `church-fathers-vi.json` (no contradictions). For Surface B: Festal Letter 39 read in full (both halves), CCC 120 verified verbatim at vatican.va, and both `sources:` URLs fetched.

**On the exile count specifically (the flagged weak point):** it holds. **Five is correct and should not be "fixed" later** — see the `confirmed` row below.

The omitted third sentence would in fact reinforce the argument (it names the 27). | `confirmed` — **no change required** | — (optional: append the third sentence) | [CCC 120, vatican.va](https://www.vatican.va/content/catechism/en/part_one/section_one/chapter_two/article_3/iv_the_canon_of_scripture.html) |
| same | 10–14 | `sources:` — two entries, both `newadvent.org/cathen/…` | **Both URLs resolve and both match their labels**: 03274a = "Canon of the New Testament" (1908), which does discuss the 367 *Epistola Festalis*; 03267a = "Canon of the Old Testament", which carries the "appointed to be read to catechumens" list. **But per the audit's own standard these are ONE source, not two** — a single 1908/1913 work, over a century old, carrying the whole canon argument of the site's flagship Sola Scriptura cluster. And 03267a **undercuts line 32 as currently written** (see above). | `contested` (sourcing depth, not accuracy) | keep both, add at least one independent modern citation and the primary text, e.g. `- label: "Athanasiô, Thư Mục Vụ 39 (năm 367) — toàn văn"` / `url: "https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/2806039.htm"` and `- label: "GLHTCG 120 (vatican.va)"` / `url: "https://www.vatican.va/content/catechism/en/part_one/section_one/chapter_two/article_3/iv_the_canon_of_scripture.html"` | [03274a](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/03274a.htm), [03267a](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/03267a.htm), [Festal Letter 39](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/2806039.htm) |

**Not checked:**
- The Greek of *De Incarnatione* 54 was verified only through a secondary citation of PG 25:192B plus the NPNF facing text; I did not open a critical edition (Thomson/SC) directly.
- `works[1]` "Orations Against the Arians": I confirmed that the **fourth** Discourse printed in the Benedictine/Migne (and New Advent) editions is now generally held spurious, and that Kannengiesser's challenge to the **third** has been widely rejected and retracted by him. The entry names no number, so nothing to correct — but if a later pass ever writes "four Orations", that is the trap.
- I did not chase Barnes's *Athanasius and Constantius* to a page number on the historicity of the Tyre bribed-woman episode; the `contested` verdict there rests on the provenance of the story (5th-c. historians only), which I did verify, plus Barnes's general methodological warning.
- I did not audit the rest of `content/giai-dap/ai-quyet-dinh-sach-nao-thuoc-kinh-thanh.md` beyond the Athanasius-dependent claims (lines 28–32), CCC 120 and the `sources:` block — the "fallible collection of infallible books" argument at line 40 and the Sola Scriptura reasoning at line 42 are theology, out of scope.
- The Nixêa/Nicêa and Constantinopolis/Constantinôpôli splits are **repo-wide**; I counted them but did not enumerate every affected file outside entry 13.

**Keep for `sources` retrofit:** [Athanasius, Festal Letter 39 — full text, New Advent](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/2806039.htm) · [Erlangen *Athanasius-Werke* project, Life and Works (dates + the five exiles)](https://www.athanasius.theologie.uni-erlangen.de/athanasius-life-and-works.html) · [NPNF2-04, *On the Incarnation* 54, CCEL](https://ccel.org/ccel/schaff/npnf204.vii.ii.liv.html)

### 14. ephrem-the-syrian — Ephrem the Syrian

**Checked:** `quote` traced to the authentic Syriac *Carmina Nisibena* XXVII, 8 via the CSCO 219 citation, and confirmed absent from the free NPNF selection; the second quotation (*Hymns against Heresies* 26) checked word-for-word against Brock's translation in *Hymns on Paradise*; the Sozomen and Palladius attributions checked against the actual chapters (Sozomen III.16, *Lausiac History* 40); Doctor status against Benedict XV's encyclical on vatican.va; the sieges (338/346/350), Jacob of Nisibis at Nicaea, the 363 cession of Nisibis, feast day, and the Ephraem Graecus / Beck transmission story against GEDSH, the New Catholic Encyclopedia and Syriac-studies sources. All four named works are genuine Syriac madrāšê cycles. The `sections[martyrdom]` cautions (not a monk in the Egyptian sense, the Basil meeting fictitious, the feigned-madness story a later invention) all check out against Amar/GEDSH and are correct as written.

| Field | Claim as written | What the sources say | Verdict | Sources |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `quote.source` | "Các Thánh Ca Nisibê, 27" / "Nisibene Hymns, 27" | The wording is genuine Ephrem and the work is one of the authentic **Syriac** collections (*Carmina Nisibena*, ed. Beck, CSCO 218–219 / 240–241), not Ephraem Graecus. But the passage is **stanza 8**, cited in the literature as *Nisibene Hymns* XXVII, 8 (= CSCO 219, p. 76). Note for the owner: the free NPNF translation on newadvent.org omits hymns 22–34 entirely, so hymn 27 is **not** findable there — anyone spot-checking will hit a dead end. | `confirmed` (quote + attribution) but add the stanza → vi: "Các Thánh Ca Nisibê, 27,8"; en: "Nisibene Hymns, 27.8" | [Missio Immaculatae, citing CSCO 219, 76](https://missiomagazine.com/mary-mirror-of-divine-beauty-in-saint-ephrem/), [NPNF Nisibene Hymns (newadvent) — contents show hymns 22–34 missing](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/3702.htm) |
| `facts[]` "Tác phẩm còn lại / Surviving works" | "4 tác phẩm / 4 works" | Wildly understated — this is just the length of the entry's own `works[]` array (the site-wide defect). GEDSH: "Nearly 400 madrāše survive, using some 50 different metrical patterns"; plus memre (six *On Faith* certainly genuine, others partly); plus genuine prose — *Commentary on Genesis* and part of *Exodus*, *Commentary on the Diatessaron*, commentaries on Acts and the Pauline letters surviving only in Armenian, and the *Prose Refutations* of Bardaiṣan, Marcion and Mani; plus artistic prose (*memrā on Our Lord*, *Letter to Publius*). The entry's own `life[]` already says "over 400 surviving hymns," so the card contradicts its own body text. | `corrected` → vi: "gần 400 bài thánh ca (madrāšê) trong nhiều chu kỳ, cùng các bài giảng thơ (memrê) và các chú giải văn xuôi"; en: "nearly 400 hymns (madrāšê) in numerous cycles, plus verse homilies (memrê) and prose commentaries" | [GEDSH, "Ephrem"](https://gedsh.bethmardutho.org/Ephrem), [OurSaints.org / Syriac Orthodox](https://www.oursaints.org/st_ephrem_page.html) |
| `sections[martyrdom]` | "he died in June 373 after **contracting the plague** while personally organizing famine relief" | The **plague** is not in the source the entry itself appeals to. Palladius, *Lausiac History* 40 (c. 419/20) says only that after the famine was over and life had returned to normal he retired to his cell and **died a month later** — no disease named, no infection contracted from those he served. Death on 9 June 373 comes from the 6th-c. *Chronicle of Edessa*, which also gives no cause. The plague detail belongs to the later legendary *vita* material the paragraph is otherwise warning against. | `unverifiable` → drop "contracting the plague"; propose vi: "…qua đời vào tháng 6 năm 373, một tháng sau khi đích thân tổ chức cứu trợ nạn đói tại Êđessa"; en: "…died in June 373, a month after personally organizing famine relief in Edessa" | [Oxford *Cult of Saints* database, E03328 (Palladius, *Lausiac Hist.* 40)](http://csla.history.ox.ac.uk/record.php?recid=E03328), [Palladius, *Lausiac History*, tertullian.org (1918 tr.)](https://www.tertullian.org/fathers/palladius_lausiac_02_text.htm) |
| `facts[]` "Tiến sĩ Hội Thánh / Doctor of the Church" | "Có / Yes" | True but bare. The declaration is precisely datable: **Benedict XV, encyclical *Principi Apostolorum Petro*, 5 October 1920** — "We confer upon St. Ephrem the Syrian, Deacon of Edessa, the title and the honors of Doctor of the Universal Church." He is the only Syriac-tradition Father so declared. | `confirmed`; recommend enriching → vi: "Có — do ĐGH Bênêđictô XV tuyên phong ngày 5-10-1920"; en: "Yes — declared by Pope Benedict XV, 5 Oct 1920" | [Benedict XV, *Principi Apostolorum Petro* (vatican.va)](https://www.vatican.va/content/benedict-xv/en/encyclicals/documents/hf_ben-xv_enc_05101920_principi-apostolorum-petro.html) |
| `role` | "Phó tế; **từ chối chức linh mục/giám mục**" / "Deacon; **declined priesthood/episcopate**" | **Deacon** is solid — Palladius (c. 419/20) already calls him "deacon of the church of Edessa," and Benedict XV's declaring sentence names him "St. Ephrem the Syrian, Deacon of Edessa." He was never a priest. But "**declined**" rests on the feigned-madness-in-the-marketplace story, which the entry's own `sections[martyrdom]` calls "most likely … a later invented explanation." The role line therefore asserts as fact what the body debunks. | `corrected` → drop the claim of active refusal: vi: "Phó tế; không bao giờ được truyền chức linh mục"; en: "Deacon; never ordained priest" | [Benedict XV, *Principi Apostolorum Petro*, 5 Oct 1920 (vatican.va)](https://www.vatican.va/content/benedict-xv/en/encyclicals/documents/hf_ben-xv_enc_05101920_principi-apostolorum-petro.html), [Oxford *Cult of Saints* E03328 (Palladius 40)](http://csla.history.ox.ac.uk/record.php?recid=E03328) |
| `sections[context]` | "resettled roughly **a hundred miles** west in Edessa" | Nisibis (Nusaybin) to Edessa (Şanlıurfa) is about **220 km / ~138 miles**; even straight-line it is well over 120 miles. "Roughly a hundred miles" understates it by a third. | `corrected` → vi: "cách đó chừng hai trăm hai mươi cây số (khoảng 140 dặm) về phía tây"; en: "roughly 220 km (about 140 miles) west" | [Nusaybin–Şanlıurfa distance (arasikackm.com)](https://www.arasikackm.com/nusaybin_sanliurfa_arasi), [Livius, "Nisibis (Nusaybin)"](https://www.livius.org/articles/place/nisibis-nusaybin/) |
| cross-file: `church-fathers.json` id 14, `martyrdom` | "died in 373 after contracting plague **while caring for plague victims** in Edessa" | The legacy file drops the famine entirely and turns the (unsupported) plague into the whole story. Palladius 40 knows only famine relief followed by death a month later. Both files need the same fix. Also note the legacy `key_quote.citation` "Nisibene Hymns, 27" carries the same missing stanza. | `corrected` (same fix as the per-slug `sections[martyrdom]` row above) | [Oxford *Cult of Saints* E03328](http://csla.history.ox.ac.uk/record.php?recid=E03328) |
| `sections[theology]` | "according to the church historian Sozomen, a rival teacher named **Bardaisan and his son Harmonius** had already set popular Syriac verse to catchy meters" | Sozomen (*Hist. Eccl.* III.16) credits the metrical innovation to **Harmonius alone** — "he was the first to subdue his native tongue to meters and musical laws" — and says Harmonius introduced his father Bardesanes' opinions into the songs. Attributing the metrical work to both men overstates Sozomen. (Ephrem's own hymns do attack Bardaisan as a songwriter, but that is a different source, not Sozomen.) | `contested` → attribute the meters to Harmonius: vi: "…con trai của Bardaisan là Harmoniô đã là người đầu tiên đặt lời thơ Syriac theo những nhịp điệu và giai điệu…, đưa vào đó các quan điểm của cha mình"; en: "…Bardaisan's son Harmonius was the first to set Syriac verse to meters and tunes, carrying his father's views into them" | [Sozomen, *Eccl. Hist.* III.16 (newadvent)](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/26023.htm), [Sozomen III.16 (orthodoxchurchfathers)](https://orthodoxchurchfathers.com/fathers/npnf202/npnf2030.html) |
| `sections[later-influence]` | "the Belgian scholar Dom Edmund Beck" | Edmund Beck (1902–1991) was **German** — a Benedictine monk of **Metten Abbey in Bavaria**, born at Huldsessen, died at Metten; studied at Munich, taught at Sant'Anselmo in Rome 1939–61. Nothing Belgian about him. (Likely confusion with the CSCO series, published at Louvain/Leuven in Belgium.) | `corrected` → vi: "học giả người Đức Dom Edmund Beck"; en: "the German scholar Dom Edmund Beck" | [GEDSH, "Beck, Edmund" (Gorgias Encycl. Dict. of the Syriac Heritage)](https://gedsh.bethmardutho.org/Beck-Edmund), [Brock, "St Ephrem: Guide to Editions" (syri.ac)](https://syri.ac/brock/ephrem) |

**Not checked:** Whether the *De Nativitate* collection as transmitted (28 hymns in Beck, CSCO 186–7) is entirely genuine — some of it consists of *soghyatha* that scholarship dates to the 5th–6th c., so the works entry "Hymns on the Nativity" may cover partly later material; I could not pin the exact authentic range (syri.ac and GEDSH both blocked direct fetches). Also not independently re-verified: `dates.born` 306 — the entry's "c. 306" is properly hedged, but note scholarship says flatly that "the often cited date of his birth, 306, cannot be substantiated," so it is a convention, not a datum. `ccc_refs` is empty (nothing to check). Vietnamese terminology: HĐGM VN's own saint-of-the-day page uses "Thánh Ephrem, phó tế tiến sĩ Hội Thánh," matching the site — no issue found.

### 15. hilary-of-poitiers — Hilary of Poitiers

**Checked:** `quote` chased to its real source — the Latin *"scripturae enim non in legendo sunt, sed in intelligendo"* is *Ad Constantium* II.9 (PL 10, 570), and I read *De Trinitate* II.3 in NPNF to confirm the sentence is not there; the two further quotations in `sections[more-quotes]` located in *De Trinitate* I.5 and I.10; dates, feast day, Doctor declaration, works list, Béziers/exile chronology and the Abra question against Britannica, the New Catholic Encyclopedia, and Watson's NPNF introduction; the legacy `church-fathers.json` record compared field-by-field.

| Field | Claim as written | What the sources say | Verdict | Sources |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `facts[]` "Tiến sĩ Hội Thánh / Doctor of the Church" + "Lễ kính / Feast day" | "Có / Yes"; "13 tháng 1 / January 13" | Doctor: declared by **Bl. Pius IX in 1851** — bare "Yes" again. Feast: January 13 is correct for the current General Roman Calendar; the New Catholic Encyclopedia notes it was **formerly January 14** (pre-1969), which is why older devotional sources give the 14th. | `confirmed`; recommend enriching → vi: "Có — do ĐGH Piô IX tuyên phong năm 1851"; en: "Yes — declared by Pope Pius IX, 1851" | [Vatican City State, "13 January: Saint Hilary of Poitiers, Bishop and Doctor"](https://www.vaticanstate.va/en/state-and-government/general-informations/saint-of-the-day/1337-13-january-saint-hilary-of-poitiers-bishop-and-doctor-of-the-church.html), [New Catholic Encyclopedia (encyclopedia.com)](https://www.encyclopedia.com/religion/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/hilary-poitiers-st) |
| `life[0]` + `apologetics[1]` | "đã kết hôn và có một con gái trước khi được truyền chức giám mục" / "married with a daughter before his episcopal ordination"; and the apologetic "Hilary's life as a married man with a daughter … shows clerical celibacy has always been a matter of Church discipline" | **The entry contradicts itself, and the apologetic rests on material the entry's own `sections[martyrdom]` calls a medieval invention.** Watson's NPNF introduction: "Hilary's legendary daughter Abra, to whom he is said to have written a letter printed in the editions of his works, is now generally abandoned by the best authorities," and of Hilary before his conversion, "His age at the time, his employment, **whether or no he was married** … can never be known." The letter *Ad Abram filiam* is regarded as a forgery. So neither the wife nor the daughter is securely attested — the argument as built cannot stand on Hilary. | `unverifiable` → hedge `life[0]` to vi: "Theo truyền thống, ngài đã kết hôn trước khi làm giám mục, nhưng đời sống gia đình của ngài không được xác nhận về mặt lịch sử [cần kiểm chứng]"; en: "Traditionally said to have been married before becoming a bishop, though his family life is not historically attested". The `apologetics[1]` claim should either be dropped or moved to a Father whose marriage IS attested. | [NPNF2-09, Watson's Introduction to Hilary (CCEL)](https://www.ccel.org/ccel/schaff/npnf209.ii.iii.i.html), [Wikipedia "Abra of Poitiers" → the hagiographical two-centuries-later attestation and forged letter](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abra_of_Poitiers) |
| `sections[martyrdom]` — the Abra caution itself | "whose existence rests entirely on hagiographical accounts written roughly two centuries after Hilary's death … a probable medieval literary invention" | **Correct, and unusually well-informed** — this matches Watson's NPNF verdict exactly. Do not let a later editor "fix" this back to the popular version. | `confirmed` (notable) | [NPNF2-09, Watson's Introduction (CCEL)](https://www.ccel.org/ccel/schaff/npnf209.ii.iii.i.html) |
| `dates` / `facts[]` "Sinh / Born" | "c. 315 — c. 367", born `315` | Genuinely contested. Britannica and the New Catholic Encyclopedia give **c. 315**; Watson's NPNF introduction says "probably about the year **300** a.d." The real spread in the literature is roughly c. 300 – c. 315, and no source gives a firm year. The `display` string is already hedged with "c.", so the exposure is the bare integer `born: 315` and any downstream "born 315" rendering. | `contested` → keep "c. 315" but consider widening, e.g. vi: "khoảng năm 300–315"; en: "c. 300–315"; do not present 315 as a bare year | [Britannica, St. Hilary of Poitiers](https://www.britannica.com/biography/Saint-Hilary-of-Poitiers), [NPNF2-09, Watson's Introduction (CCEL)](https://www.ccel.org/ccel/schaff/npnf209.ii.iii.i.html), [New Catholic Encyclopedia (encyclopedia.com)](https://www.encyclopedia.com/religion/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/hilary-poitiers-st) |
| `sections[more-quotes]` | "Hilary opens De Trinitate with an unusually personal passage describing **his own pre-Christian intellectual search**, a kind of philosophical autobiography" | The passage is real, but scholarship does not read it as straight autobiography. Watson: "we cannot … accept it, as it stands, as the record of actual experience," because "it is too symmetrical in its advance … and too well prepared as a piece of literary workmanship." It is a constructed literary account of a spiritual itinerary. | `contested` → hedge, e.g. vi: "…một đoạn văn mang tính cá nhân khác thường trình bày hành trình tri thức của ngài — mà các học giả coi là một tác phẩm văn chương được dàn dựng công phu hơn là một hồi ký theo nghĩa đen"; en: "…which scholars read as a carefully composed literary account of a spiritual itinerary rather than a literal memoir" | [NPNF2-09, Watson's Introduction (CCEL)](https://www.ccel.org/ccel/schaff/npnf209.ii.iii.i.html) |
| `sections[martyrdom]` + cross-file `church-fathers.json` `nickname` | "Búa Đập Phái Ariô (Malleus Arianorum)" / "Hammer of the Arians"; legacy `nickname` also "The Athanasius of the West" | I could not trace **either** epithet to a named ancient author. Every source I found asserts he "is sometimes called" / "has often been referred to as" one or both, without attribution; none names Jerome, Augustine, or any contemporary. So the entry's caution ("a retrospective title rather than a name used of him by his own contemporaries") is directionally right but is itself unsourced — and the same caveat applies to "Athanasius of the West," which the legacy file presents as a plain nickname with no hedge at all. | `unverifiable` → keep the hedge in the per-slug entry; add the same hedge to the legacy `nickname` field, or mark `[cần kiểm chứng]` | [Britannica, St. Hilary of Poitiers](https://www.britannica.com/biography/Saint-Hilary-of-Poitiers), [Marians of the Immaculate Conception, "Hammer of the Arians"](https://marian.org/articles/hammer-arians-st-hilary-poitiers) |
| cross-file: `church-fathers.json` id 15 | `key_quote.citation` = "De Trinitate, II.3"; `martyrdom` = "Died naturally in 367, **restored to his see**" | (i) The same wrong citation is duplicated in the legacy file — fixing only the per-slug file leaves the homepage/search surfaces wrong. (ii) "Restored to his see" **contradicts the per-slug entry**, which says Constantius sent him home "without ever formally restoring him to communion." The per-slug reading is the better one: Hilary was returned to Gaul as a troublemaker, not rehabilitated; he simply resumed his duties. | `corrected` → citation to "Ad Constantium Augustum, II.9"; and change "restored to his see" to "returned to Gaul in 360 and resumed his episcopal duties at Poitiers" | [NPNF2-09, Watson's Introduction (CCEL)](https://www.ccel.org/ccel/schaff/npnf209.ii.iii.i.html), [Britannica, St. Hilary of Poitiers](https://www.britannica.com/biography/Saint-Hilary-of-Poitiers) |
| `facts[]` "Tác phẩm còn lại / Surviving works" | "2 tác phẩm / 2 works" | Again just the length of `works[]` (site-wide defect). The New Catholic Encyclopedia lists, as surviving: *De Trinitate* (12 books), *De synodis*, *Ad Constantium* and *Contra Constantium*, *Contra Auxentium*, *Tractatus super Matthaeum*, *Tractatus super Psalmos*, *Liber mysteriorum* (partially preserved), *Fragmenta ex opere historico* (the *Collectanea antiariana*), and three incomplete hymns — Jerome already noted only fragments of the hymns survive. That is roughly nine works, not two. | `corrected` → vi: "khoảng 9 tác phẩm còn lại, gồm De Trinitate, De Synodis, các chú giải Mátthêu và Thánh Vịnh, các tác phẩm chống Constantiô và Auxentiô, cùng vài mảnh thánh thi"; en: "about nine surviving works, including De Trinitate, De Synodis, commentaries on Matthew and the Psalms, the works against Constantius and Auxentius, and fragments of hymns" | [New Catholic Encyclopedia, "Hilary of Poitiers, St." (encyclopedia.com)](https://www.encyclopedia.com/religion/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/hilary-poitiers-st), [Britannica, "St. Hilary of Poitiers"](https://www.britannica.com/biography/Saint-Hilary-of-Poitiers) |
| `quote.source` (and the same citation implied in `apologetics[0]`) | "Về Ba Ngôi, II.3" / "De Trinitate, II.3" | **Wrong work.** The line is Latin *"scripturae enim non in legendo sunt, sed in intelligendo"* and comes from Hilary's ***Ad Constantium Augustum*, lib. II, cap. 9** (PL 10, 570) — the 359 address to the emperor, not *De Trinitate*. (Jerome repeats the phrase at *Dial. contra Luciferianos* 28.) I checked *De Trinitate* II.3 in the NPNF translation: it argues the adjacent point — "Heresy lies in the sense assigned, not in the word written; the guilt is that of the expositor, not of the text" — which is almost certainly how the misattribution arose, but it does **not** contain the sentence quoted. The wording itself is genuine Hilary. | `corrected` → vi: "Gửi hoàng đế Constantiô, quyển II, 9"; en: "Ad Constantium Augustum, II.9" | [Florovsky, "The Function of Tradition in the Ancient Church," with the PL citation](https://www.fatheralexander.org/booklets/english/church_tradition_florovsky.htm), [Hilary, *De Trinitate* II (NPNF, newadvent) — §3 does not contain it](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/330202.htm) |

**Not checked:** The Council of Seleucia (359) — Hilary attended it during his exile and it is a load-bearing event for the `De Synodis` / homoiousian story the entry tells, but the entry never mentions it, so there was no claim to verify; flagging only that its absence leaves `sections[context]`/`later-influence` thinner than the sources warrant. I also did not verify the specific claim in `sections[theology]` that *De Trinitate* imported the `ousia`/`hypostasis` distinction into Latin, nor the `later-influence` claim of a direct debt from Ambrose and Augustine to Hilary — both are standard in the handbooks but I ran out of budget before checking Beckwith. Whether Hilary's release was 360 or 361 is given differently by different sources (Britannica: exile 356–360; another gives release 361); the entry's 360 is within range. `ccc_refs` empty.

**Keep for `sources` retrofit:** [Watson's Introduction to Hilary, NPNF2-09 (CCEL)](https://www.ccel.org/ccel/schaff/npnf209.ii.iii.i.html) · [New Catholic Encyclopedia, "Hilary of Poitiers, St." (encyclopedia.com)](https://www.encyclopedia.com/religion/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/hilary-poitiers-st) · [Hilary, *De Trinitate*, NPNF translation (newadvent.org/fathers/3302.htm)](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/3302.htm)

**Cross-entry note (terminology, both entries):** the site is internally inconsistent on the Vietnamese form of Nicaea — `content/` has **63** occurrences of "Nixêa" against **47** of "Nicêa" (the Catechism body `content/content.json` uses "Nicêa" exclusively, 12/12, while these Giáo Phụ entries use "Nixêa"). Both forms are attested at HĐGM VN, so neither is wrong; the inconsistency is. Not a fact error — flagged for a separate normalization pass, not for this audit's edit round.

---

### 16. cyril-of-jerusalem — Cyril of Jerusalem

**Checked:** Both quotations traced to their actual chapters in the NPNF2-07 full text at New Advent
(headline quote = *Myst. Cat.* 4 §6 = Cat. Lect. 22 §6; Golgotha/True Cross quote = Cat. Lect. 4 §10) —
both are real and correctly cited. Dates, episcopal accession, the three exiles and the Constantinople-381
question checked against Benedict XVI's 2007 catechesis on Cyril at vatican.va, the *New Catholic
Encyclopedia* (via Encyclopedia.com), Wace's *Dictionary of Christian Biography*, and Drijvers,
*Cyril of Jerusalem: Bishop and City* (Brill 2004); EWTN's saint article was used but is a reprint of the
1913 *Catholic Encyclopedia* and is counted as **one** source with it, not two. The *Mystagogic Catecheses*
authorship question checked against the Doval monograph (CUA Press 2001) and the JTS review of it. The
surviving-works corpus checked against vatican.va, the CUA Press 2-volume edition, and the NPNF2-07 table
of contents. Vietnamese forms grepped against `content/content.json`. Both `refs_ccc` paragraphs (957,
2683) fetched at vatican.va. Cross-checked `church-fathers.json` / `church-fathers-vi.json` (id 16).

entries 1 and 2. The exposure here is of a different shape: an *unsourced* assertion, resting (for Cyril)
on a text whose authorship is disputed.

  remaining unchecked factual assertion on that page — someone should own it.
- The `sections[].martyrdom` arithmetic "fifteen to sixteen of his roughly thirty-six years… away from
  his see": the three exiles as dated (357–359, 360–361, 367–378) total roughly **14–15** years, so the
  figure is at the high end of what the chronology supports. It is hedged ("something on the order of"),
  so I did not pursue a scholarly total; flagging it here so it is not mistaken for verified.

**Keep for `sources` retrofit:**
- [Benedict XVI, General Audience, 27 June 2007 — St Cyril of Jerusalem (vatican.va)](https://www.vatican.va/content/benedict-xvi/en/audiences/2007/documents/hf_ben-xvi_aud_20070627.html) — the single best authoritative source for this entry: birth year, ordination, all three exiles with years, the 24 catecheses, Constantinople 381 and the 382 synodal letter.
- [NPNF2-07, Cyril of Jerusalem, *Catechetical Lectures* (New Advent)](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/3101.htm) — full text; Lecture 4 §10 (True Cross), Lecture 22 §6 (headline quote), Lecture 23 §9 (the Giải Đáp witness).
- [New Catholic Encyclopedia, "Cyril of Jerusalem" (Encyclopedia.com)](https://www.encyclopedia.com/environment/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/cyril-jerusalem) — modern reference, independent of the 1913 CE, and explicit on the Mystagogic authorship split.

### 17. gregory-of-nazianzus — Gregory of Nazianzus

**Checked:** Headline `quote` traced to the NPNF II.7 text of *Ep.* 101 ("To Cledonius the Priest Against Apollinarius") and matches verbatim; both further quotations in `sections[more-quotes]` located in the Greek-derived NPNF text and verified word for word (Oration 40.41 for the "One / the Three / one torch" passage, Oration 43.20 for "one soul, inhabiting two bodies") — but the *context* claim attached to the second is wrong (row below). Dates checked against the 1913 Catholic Encyclopedia and the New Catholic Encyclopedia; the Constantinople I sequence against Tanner's *Decrees* and the CE; `works[]` and the surviving-works count against the New Catholic Encyclopedia and a Yale library guide; Vietnamese name forms against the site's own `content/content.json` plus TGP Sài Gòn and HĐGM VN; cross-checked both legacy combined datasets. The Hypsistarian conversion of Gregory the Elder, the Five Theological Orations of 380 at Constantinople, *De Vita Sua*, the peaceful death in retirement, the "Three Holy Hierarchs" veneration and the 2 January feast in the current General Roman Calendar all check out and are not listed below.

| Field | Claim as written | What the sources say | Verdict | Sources |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| `life[1]` | "Chủ tọa Công đồng Constantinôpôli I (381) … Từ nhiệm chức vụ không lâu **sau đó** vì hòa bình." / "Presided over the First Council of Constantinople (381) … Resigned his see shortly **after** for the sake of peace." | The order is wrong twice over. Gregory did **not** preside from the opening: **Meletius of Antioch** presided first; Gregory took charge only **on Meletius' sudden death mid-council**; and he resigned **during the council, not after it** — **Nectarius** was then baptised, consecrated and presided "until its closure." Tanner's *Decrees*: "Meletius … at first presided over the council. Then on Meletius's sudden death, Gregory of Nazianzus took charge … Gregory resigned the episcopacy and Nectarius … presided over the council until its closure." | `corrected` → propose: vi "Sau khi Mêlêtiô thành Antiôkia — vị chủ tọa đầu tiên — qua đời giữa công đồng, Grêgôriô tiếp quản việc chủ tọa Công đồng Constantinôpôli I (381); ngài từ nhiệm **ngay trong lúc công đồng còn đang họp**, vì hòa bình, và Nectariô chủ tọa cho đến khi bế mạc." / en "After Meletius of Antioch, the council's first president, died mid-session, Gregory took over the presidency of the First Council of Constantinople (381); he resigned **while the council was still sitting**, for the sake of peace, and Nectarius presided to its close." | [Tanner, *Decrees of the Ecumenical Councils*, Constantinople I intro (papalencyclicals.net)](https://www.papalencyclicals.net/councils/ecum02.htm), [Catholic Encyclopedia, First Council of Constantinople](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/04308a.htm) |
| `role` | "Giám mục Sasima (một thời gian ngắn)" / "Bishop of Sasima (**briefly**)" | "Briefly" implies he held and exercised the see for a short time. New Catholic Encyclopedia: Basil "prevailed upon Gregory, much against his will … to accept consecration as its bishop. Gregory did so, but never took possession of the see." | `corrected` → propose: vi "Được tấn phong giám mục Sasima nhưng chưa bao giờ nhận tòa" / en "Consecrated bishop of Sasima but never took possession of the see" | [New Catholic Encyclopedia via encyclopedia.com, "Gregory of Nazianzus, St."](https://www.encyclopedia.com/religion/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/gregory-nazianzus-st), [Catholic Encyclopedia, St. Gregory of Nazianzus](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/07010b.htm) |
| `dates.died` / `dates.display` / `facts[]` "Qua đời / Died" | "390"; "25 tháng 1, năm 390" / "January 25, 390 AD" — stated flat, no hedge | The **year is contested**, not settled. The 1913 CE: the date of his death "is unknown, but it may be assigned with tolerable certainty to the year 389 or 390." New Catholic Encyclopedia hedges it as "d. Arianzus, **c.** 390." The **day** (25 January) is well attested and can stand; the year cannot stand bare. | `contested` → propose: `dates.display` "c. 329 — c. 390"; `facts` value vi "25 tháng 1, khoảng năm 389/390" / en "25 January, c. 389/390". (Leave `dates.died: 390` as the sort key.) | [Catholic Encyclopedia, St. Gregory of Nazianzus](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/07010b.htm), [New Catholic Encyclopedia via encyclopedia.com](https://www.encyclopedia.com/religion/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/gregory-nazianzus-st) |
| `facts[]` "Tác phẩm còn lại / Surviving works" | "1 tác phẩm" / "**1 work**" | Badly wrong — this is the site-wide defect (the value is just `works[].length`). New Catholic Encyclopedia: **44 orations survive** (Or. 35 being spurious), **244 letters** were published (three spurious), and the poetry runs to **more than 16,000 lines**. The Five Theological Orations listed in `works[]` are Orations 27–31 — five items *inside* the 44, not the whole corpus. (The corpus is transmitted as **45 numbered orations and 245 letters**; NCE's 44/244 are the same corpus minus the spurious items — so quote whichever, but do not mix them.) | `corrected` → propose: vi "45 bài giảng được lưu truyền (44 bài được coi là đích thực; Or. 35 bị coi là ngụy tác), 245 lá thư, và hơn 16.000 câu thơ" / en "45 transmitted orations (44 regarded as genuine; Or. 35 spurious), 245 letters, and over 16,000 lines of poetry" | [New Catholic Encyclopedia via encyclopedia.com](https://www.encyclopedia.com/religion/encyclopedias-almanacs-transcripts-and-maps/gregory-nazianzus-st), [Catholic Encyclopedia, St. Gregory of Nazianzus](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/07010b.htm), [Yale University Library research guide, Gregory of Nazianzus](https://guides.library.yale.edu/ancientchristianity/gregorynazianzus) |
| `sections[more-quotes]` | Of "We seemed to have one soul inhabiting two bodies": "trong bối cảnh nguyên thủy, nó **khép lại một đoạn văn than tiếc** về việc sự hiệp nhất thuở ban đầu ấy đã bị thử thách đến mức nào…" / "in its original context it **closes a passage grieving** how far that early unity had been tested by the very Church both men had given their lives to serve." | The line is **Oration 43.20**, and its context is the opposite of what the entry says — §20 is an unbroken celebration of the Athens years. It **opens** the passage rather than closing a lament: "Such were our feelings for each other, when we had thus supported, as Pindar has it, our well-built chamber with pillars of gold… We seemed to have one soul, inhabiting two bodies… We struggled, not each to gain the first place for himself, but to yield it to the other." No grief over later strain appears anywhere near it. | `corrected` → drop the context claim; propose vi "Câu này nằm trong Bài Giảng 43, số 20, giữa một đoạn ca ngợi những năm hai người cùng học tại Athens — nói về sự hiệp nhất thuở ban đầu ấy, trước những căng thẳng về sau." / en "The line comes at Oration 43.20, inside a passage celebrating their student years at Athens — describing that early unity, before the later strain." | [NPNF II.7, Gregory Nazianzen, Oration 43 §20 (newadvent)](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/310243.htm), [Cambridge Edition of Early Christian Writings, Oration 43 (contents/sectioning)](https://www.cambridge.org/core/books/abs/cambridge-edition-of-early-christian-writings/gregory-of-nazianzus-oration-431-3738-4353-6070-7780-funeral-oration-for-basil-the-great-bishop-of-cappadocian-caesarea/033CC99485998CBB11E5FC64454C8BB5) |

| `life[1]` + `sections[later-influence]` | "hoàn thiện Kinh Tin Kính Nixêa-Constantinôpôli" / "**finalizing** the Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed"; and "Kinh Tin Kính … được hoàn thiện tại công đồng ngài chủ tọa" | Stated as settled fact, but this is one of the genuinely contested questions in the field. Tanner's *Decrees*: no original doctrinal decisions of the council survive; scholars dispute whether it composed a new creed at all, and **"no mention is made of this creed by ancient witnesses until the council of Chalcedon"** (451) — Chalcedon is what retroactively attached it to the 150 fathers of 381. The 1913 CE goes further, reporting a case that the text was "probably composed (369–73) by St. Cyril of Jerusalem" and only *adopted*, and citing Harnack for the post-Chalcedon attribution. | `contested` → propose hedging: vi "gắn liền với Kinh Tin Kính Nixêa-Constantinôpôli — bản kinh mà truyền thống quy cho công đồng này, dù các nhà nghiên cứu vẫn tranh luận về việc công đồng soạn hay chỉ chuẩn nhận nó [cần kiểm chứng thêm]" / en "associated with the Nicene-Constantinopolitan Creed — the creed tradition ascribes to this council, though scholars still debate whether the council composed it or only received it" | [Tanner, *Decrees of the Ecumenical Councils*, Constantinople I intro](https://www.papalencyclicals.net/councils/ecum02.htm), [Catholic Encyclopedia, First Council of Constantinople](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/04308a.htm) |
| `facts[]` "Tiến sĩ Hội Thánh / Doctor of the Church" | "Có" / "Yes" — bare, no year or pope | The declaration is documented and specific, and the audit spec asks for it: Gregory was named a Doctor of the universal Church by **Pope St. Pius V in the 1568 revision of the Breviary**, together with the other three Greek Doctors (Athanasius, Basil, John Chrysostom). Not an error — a missing datum. | `confirmed` (enrich) → propose vi "Có — Đức Piô V công bố năm 1568, cùng ba Tiến sĩ Hy Lạp còn lại" / en "Yes — declared by Pope St. Pius V in 1568, with the other three Greek Doctors" | [Catholic Encyclopedia, Doctors of the Church](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/05075a.htm), [Catholic Culture library, The Doctors of the Church](https://www.catholicculture.org/culture/library/view.cfm?recnum=7236) |
| `name.vi` / `role.vi` / all `vi` prose — **Vietnamese terminology** | "Grêgôriô thành **Nazianzô**" (also `region_see` "Nazianzô, Cappađôcia") | **Contradicts the site's own content.** `content/content.json` (the Vietnamese Catechism text already shipping on this site) uses **"Thánh Grêgôriô thành Nazien"** in all three places it names him (lines ~1536, ~4104, ~9534). And no Vietnamese Catholic authority I checked uses "Nazianzô" — the attested forms are **"Grêgôriô Nazianzênô"** (TGP Sài Gòn, GP Long Xuyên), **"Grêgôriô Nazian"** (HĐGM VN, GP Long Xuyên) and **"Grêgôriô Nadien"** (TGP Sài Gòn). Three attested spellings exist; "Nazianzô" is not among them, and the site is internally inconsistent with itself. | `corrected` → align on one attested form. Recommend **"Grêgôriô Nazianzênô"** (the form the liturgical-calendar pages use for the 2 Jan memorial) with the site's own "thành Nazien" as the in-prose variant — but at minimum stop using "Nazianzô". | [`content/content.json` (site's own Catechism text)](D:/Dropbox/Claude/MinhofGod%20Websites/Hoi%20Dap%20Cong%20Giao%20Website/content/content.json), [TGP Sài Gòn, "Ngày 02/01: Thánh Basiliô Cả và thánh Grêgôriô Nazianzênô"](https://tgpsaigon.net/bai-viet/ngay-02-01-thanh-basilio-ca-va-thanh-gregorio-nazianzeno-30228), [HĐGM VN, "Ngày 2 tháng Giêng: Thánh Basiliô Cả … và Thánh Grêgôriô Nazian"](https://hdgmvietnam.com/chi-tiet/ngay-2-thang-gieng-thanh-basilio-ca-329%E2%80%93379-va-thanh-gregorio-nazian-325%E2%80%93390-giam-muc-tien-si-hoi-thanh-18934) |

**Not checked:** The date and occasion of Oration 40 and Oration 43 (Or. 43 is usually placed in 382, three years after Basil's death, but I did not verify that and the entry does not state it). Whether the entry's "returned to Cappadocia in the late 350s" is right to the year. ### 18. basil-of-caesarea — Basil of Caesarea

**Checked:** Headline `quote` — the citation is **correct**: the homily is Basil's Homily 6, *In illud dictum evangelii secundum Lucam, "Destruam horrea mea"* (PG 31.261–277), i.e. a homily on Luke 12:18, and the four-clause chain quoted in `sections[more-quotes]` matches the attested text (Schroeder's 2009 Popular Patristics translation renders it "The bread you are holding back is for the hungry, the clothes you keep put away are for the naked, the shoes that are rotting away with disuse are for those who have none, the silver you keep buried in the earth is for the needy" — the entry's version is a different but genuine English rendering of the same passage, not a misattribution). Dates, the 370 election and the surviving-works figures checked against the 1913 Catholic Encyclopedia; the Basiliad against Gregory of Nazianzus *Oration* 43.63 in a full text (which confirms both the "new city" language *and* the lepers detail, and confirms the entry's own honest caveat that Gregory is the source); the Rufinus→Benedict transmission against the *Regula Basilii* (Rufinus, 397) and the Rule of Benedict (c. 530), which makes "roughly 150 years later" defensible; Vietnamese forms against HĐGM VN, TGP Sài Gòn and GP Vĩnh Long; both legacy combined datasets cross-checked.

**Task-specific checks that came back clean, so no row below:** (a) **Constantinople I (381)** — the entry is *correct and unusually careful* here: it states plainly that Basil died before the council and did not live to see it, and nowhere implies he attended. Council dates verified: opened May 381, closed 9 July 381. (b) **The Cappadocian relationships** — "Anh của Grêgôriô thành Nyssa và Macrina Trẻ" / brother of Gregory of Nyssa and Macrina the Younger, and the ten children of Basil the Elder and Emmelia, are confirmed by the CE ("became the father of ten children"); and the entry does **not** present Sasima as happy collaboration — no. 17's `later-influence` section explicitly calls it "a lasting estrangement that Gregory referred to bitterly", which the sources support. (c) The *Hexaemeron*, *On the Holy Spirit*, and the Basiliad are all correctly attributed. (e) See the surviving-works row.

The election year (370) is a basic datum the entry omits entirely. | `corrected` → propose vi "gần chín năm làm giám mục (được tấn phong năm 370)" / en "his nearly nine years as bishop (consecrated in 370)"; and add the election to `life[]` or `facts[]`. | [Catholic Encyclopedia, St. Basil the Great](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/02330b.htm) |

Against the council's close the gap is 2 years 6 months. The phrase is right only for the opening. | `corrected` → propose the unambiguous form: vi "qua đời khoảng hai năm rưỡi trước khi công đồng khai mạc" / en "dying roughly two and a half years before the council opened" | [Tanner, *Decrees of the Ecumenical Councils*, Constantinople I (dates of the council)](https://www.papalencyclicals.net/councils/ecum02.htm), [Catholic Encyclopedia, First Council of Constantinople](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/04308a.htm) |
| `dates.died` / `dates.display` / `facts[]` "Qua đời / Died" | "379"; "1 tháng 1, năm 379" / "January 1, 379 AD" — stated flat | 1 January 379 is the **traditional and still-majority** date (1913 CE: "died 1 January, 379"), and the entry is not wrong to use it. | `sections[theology]` | Basil's silence was "được chính bạn ngài, Grêgôriô thành Nazianzô, biện minh công khai như một 'sự khôn khéo mục vụ'" / "defended explicitly by his friend Gregory of Nazianzus as a deliberate 'economy'" — no citation given | **Substantially right, but under-cited and slightly over-simple.** Verified: Basil nowhere writes "the Spirit is God"; Gregory does address those "who blamed Basil for refraining from the express assertion 'The Holy Ghost is God'", and the NPNF apparatus to *Oration 43* files that discussion under the heading **"Economy"** and notes that Basil's conduct "has the approval of Athanasius" (Athanasius, *Letter to Palladius*). What the entry flattens is that Gregory was not simply Basil's defender throughout — the correspondence around this (Gregory *Ep.* 58; Basil *Epp.* 113–114) shows friction over exactly this reticence before the public defence in the funeral oration. **I could not fetch Oration 43 §§68–69 directly this session** (every full-text host truncated before that section), so the section numbers are from secondary indices, not my own reading. | `confirmed` (with caveat) → add the citation, and hedge the "defended" into its real shape: propose adding "— trong bài điếu văn cho Basiliô (Bài Giảng 43), sau khi chính Grêgôriô đã có lúc phàn nàn với Basiliô về sự dè dặt này (Thư 58)" / en "— in his funeral oration for Basil (Oration 43), after Gregory himself had at one point pressed Basil about that same reticence (Letter 58)" | [NPNF II.7, Oration 43 and its notes](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/310243.htm), [Athanasius, Letter to Palladius (NPNF II.4)](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/2806063.htm) |
| Cross-file (`church-fathers.json` / `church-fathers-vi.json`, id 18) | — | **No contradiction** with the per-slug file — the legacy records repeat it word for word (same key_quote and citation, same "January 1, 379 AD", same three key_writings). So the "3 works"/missing-370 fixes must land in **three** files. One extra note: the legacy `key_quote` truncates the chain after "…him who is naked", while the per-slug `more-quotes` section carries the full four-clause version — that is a difference in extent, not a contradiction. | `confirmed` (consistency) — note for the fixer | — |

**Not present in the entry (not errors — flagged because the task asked):**

- **The Modestus confrontation is missing entirely.** It is one of the best-attested episodes in Basil's life, and it has a primary source: Gregory of Nazianzus, *Oration* 43 §§48–50, where the praetorian prefect Modestus threatens confiscation, banishment, torture and death and Basil replies that "a man who has nothing is beyond the reach of confiscation… Banishment is impossible for me… Death is my benefactor, for it will send me the sooner to God," after which Modestus reports to Valens, "No one has ever yet spoken thus, and with such boldness, to Modestus." If it is added, that is the citation. ([NPNF II.7, Oration 43](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/310243.htm))
- **The *Philocalia* (the Origen anthology) is absent from both no. 17 and no. 18** — and if it is ever added, it must be **hedged, not asserted**. The traditional attribution (Basil and Gregory compiled it, probably during the Pontus retreat) is stated in the Greek text itself and is still the majority view — the 1913 CE says flatly that they "published a selection of [Origen's writings] called the 'Philocalia'" — but scholarly opinion is divided: É. Junod, editor of chapters 21–27 in *Sources Chrétiennes*, accepts both as compilers, while M. Harl, editor of chapters 1–20 in the same series, questions it, as do others. Correct treatment: "theo truyền thống do Basiliô và Grêgôriô biên soạn (một số học giả hiện đại đặt nghi vấn)". ([Catholic Encyclopedia, St. Basil the Great](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/02330b.htm), [Oxford Reference, "Philocalia"](https://www.oxfordreference.com/display/10.1093/oi/authority.20110803100323104))

**Not checked:** The dates of composition for *On the Holy Spirit* and the *Hexaemeron* (`works[].date` is empty in all three entries, so there is nothing to be wrong — but nothing verified either). The claim in `sections[context]` that Macrina the Elder "survived hiding in the wilderness during an earlier persecution" (plausible and CE-consistent, but I did not trace it to Basil *Ep.* 223). The claim that Macrina the Younger was the *eldest* sibling. The claim in `sections[martyrdom]` that the chronic illness was "probably liver disease" — I did not verify this and it should be treated as unverified. Whether the Longer/Shorter Rules are "the single most influential document in the entire history of Eastern monasticism" is evaluative and out of scope. `ccc_refs` is empty.

**Keep for `sources` retrofit:** [Catholic Encyclopedia, St. Basil the Great](https://www.newadvent.org/cathen/02330b.htm) · [Gregory of Nazianzus, *Oration* 43 (Funeral Oration on Basil) — NPNF II.7, incl. §§48–50 Modestus and §63 the Basiliad](https://www.newadvent.org/fathers/310243.htm) · [C. P. Schroeder, trans., *St Basil the Great: On Social Justice*, Popular Patristics 38 (SVS Press, 2009) — the modern critical translation of Homily 6](https://svspress.com/blog/review-on-social-justice/)

---

### Cross-entry note (entries 17–18)

The two most consequential findings are both in **no. 17**, and both are also sitting in `church-fathers.json` and `church-fathers-vi.json` in identical wording:

1. "**Presided over the First Council of Constantinople (381) … Resigned his see shortly after**" gets the sequence backwards. Meletius presided first and died mid-council; Gregory took over; Gregory resigned **during** the council; Nectarius presided to the close. As written it reads as though Gregory ran the council start to finish and then stepped down afterwards.
2. "**Bishop of Sasima (briefly)**" — he never took possession of Sasima at all. "Briefly" turns a consecration he resented and never acted on into a short episcopate he actually served, which also softens the very Basil/Gregory rupture the entry's own `later-influence` section is honest about.

### 19. gregory-of-nyssa — Gregory of Nyssa

**Checked:** `quote` traced to the Malherbe–Ferguson translation of *The Life of Moses*; the `more-quotes` "Concepts create idols" debunking re-verified against Maspero and Moltmann's own citation; dates, feast day and Doctor-of-the-Church status against the Vatican City State saint-of-the-day page and the Catholic Encyclopedia (flagged where the 1913 CE is out of date); the 372 consecration, 376 deposition and 378 return against two encyclopedias; all 3 `works[]` checked for existence and authorship (no spuria); `ccc_refs` empty; Vietnamese forms grepped against `content/content.json`; cross-checked against `church-fathers.json` / `church-fathers-vi.json` id 19. Full detail and caveats in the shared block at the end of this fragment.
